Build: each ingredient gets a `kind` in core; the category menu's component generalizes to a menu of grouped choices rather than a copy (rule 4), the radios in one group per heading so ↑ and ↓ run on through them; `data-tool="ingredient"` so the toolbar's arrows reach it (ADR 0014). As a trial in its own commit, for Sean to try on his iPhone. UX-SPEC §1, §6; DESIGN-SYSTEM (Fluid shells, the component table); browser tests (opening, choosing, keys, axe in every theme) |
| Cooking's figures: decide each by weighing several sources, cite them and how the figure was reached, show the sources on About; very close, cross-referenced and validated                                                                                                                                                             | A sourcing pass, `docs/research/cooking-2.md`. For each ingredient, every figure we can find for a US cup, with the measuring method each states (spooned and leveled, scooped, packed, sifted): USDA FoodData Central SR Legacy and FNDDS (its own portion weights), USDA's HG-72 (from SR 13, so not independent), US nutrition labels (21 CFR 101.9 makes each give a household measure with its gram weight, "1/4 cup (30 g)" on a flour bag is 120 g a cup), King Arthur Baking's ingredient weight chart, and America's Test Kitchen where published. The figure kept is the one for the method recipes mean (spooned and leveled for flour, packed for brown sugar): USDA's where it falls within the method-consistent sources' spread, else their median, with why. Two reviewers gather sources blind to each other, then reconcile; a third checks every citation. Each ingredient then carries its figure, the range across sources, the method, and its sources; the About page's Cooking group lists them ("125 g, sources 120 to 125: ..."), and the line under Cooking's table names the method. ADR 0021 amended: densities have no defining standard (ADR 0020), so the figure is the measured consensus for the stated method. Golden rows and core's tests follow the figures                  |
| "Decimals" and "Fractions" read as "Decimal" and "Fraction": better logic?                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | Yes: each choice names a format, as "Symbol only" and "Symbol and name" name a way of writing names; "Decimal", "Fraction", "Both". `SettingsPanels.tsx` (the Format choices), UX-SPEC §1 and §7, the settings tests that pick them (`setSettings(page, 'Fraction')`, `/^Decimal$/`)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |

Other suggestions, for Sean with the above: Cooking's metric cup (250 mL) for visitors outside the US, now that the menu will make the cup's meaning visible; and, once the figures come from several sources, the line under Cooking's table naming the measuring method.

**Built the same day** (Sean, after the compaction: "proceed with the next steps"; yes to retiring the lab and to the ingredient menu as a trial; open to pushback), each on the gate, pushed, and passing CI, with one more note of his built on the way:

- **The formulas' tools on the left edge, and each category's name sticky** (`dcf0065`, his addition: "just like we have the sticky for show all/hide all, can we also do the same for the category titles"). Show all takes the categories' own look (`DISCLOSURE`), its chevron over theirs, Copy all as text after it; the heading and its lines stay centered. An open category's name stays under the bar while its units scroll past, until the next one's takes its place, at the bar's measured height. Opening or closing a category, or Show all and Hide all, keeps the category being read where it sits on the screen, so one closed from under the bar stays there rather than the page jumping on past it (WebKit too; a learning). The gate had failed on a doc's formatting and the commit went out anyway, read from a background run's exit code, which was `echo`'s: CI caught it, `129056f` fixed it, and a memory now says to commit only in the same command as the gate.
- **The sketchbook's snap to the right** (`1a9d8eb`, his note mid-round: "when a transition finishes it kind of snaps to the right... mainly on mobile"). Found by measurement, not guessed: each change frozen a hair before its end and screenshotted against the settled tile, in Chromium and WebKit. Every moving effect matched but Doors in WebKit: the door hinged on the tile's right edge, turned through a `perspective()` of its own, stayed a quarter open to the end, then vanished as the tile settled. The doors now take their depth from the tile's `perspective`, are clipped to it, and fade over the second half of their swing; the last frame matches the settled tile in both engines (a learning, and a unit test of Doors' keyframes). The tiles also sit on fractional pixels on an iPhone (the middle at x = 140.656), which measured no jump in either engine; if a snap remains on his iPhone, that is the next suspect.
- **Cooking's box on one line** (`0925f7c`, and `929e2d9` for its test). The wrap is now the field fit's last step, measured: one line, the unit's name giving way, the number's sizes, and only where the number fits at no size beside both pills do they take a line of their own (`data-wrap`). "123 fl oz of all-purpose flour" is one line on a desktop; a phone wraps where it must. An empty box keeps its placeholder's room. The test first pinned the 21-digit number to one line at 1280 px, which held in Windows' fonts but not CI's Linux ones (the themes use system fonts): it now uses a number that fits in any font, checked with Verdana and Courier New injected.
- **Decimal and Fraction** (`7f86070`), the format's choices in the singular.
- **The categories lab retired** (`201461f`): its entry in the retired labs index with four screenshots, the last its Cooking mocks; it lived until `7f86070`.
- **Choosing an ingredient without typing** (`580d888`, then the trial `396484a`). Each ingredient has a kind in core, and Cooking with none chosen lists them under eight headings: Flours, Sugars, Fats and oils, Dairy and eggs, Liquids and syrups, Baking, Grains, Mix-ins and spreads (eggs joined dairy rather than a heading of one, water joined the syrups, peanut butter the mix-ins). The Ingredient menu was built in the toolbar as planned and measured before shipping: it took the toolbar from one line to two on a desktop in Cooking and overran a phone's window (the ingredient's name broken over three lines, Display cut off). So the trial is the systemic version instead: pressing the ingredient's pill, which now carries a chevron, lists every ingredient in the table's place, the box's popup, the one in the box marked and the cursor on it; taking one keeps the amount and the cup; Escape, typing, or the pill again closes it. The chevron gives way after the unit's name where the box is narrow. Revert `396484a` to drop the trial; the grouping stays. A learning: "A picker for a value in the box belongs in the box's popup, not the toolbar."
- **No hairline between the two sticky headers** (`1da887c`, his note mid-round: "a very small space between the 2 headers when both sticky where i see some of the scrolling text"). Each sticky layer is rounded to the screen's pixels on its own; the category names now stick a pixel under the bar, at its measured height. Checked by the seam's pixels at scales 1, 1.25, 1.5, and 3 in Chromium and WebKit: text showed in WebKit at 1.5 before, nowhere now (a learning).
- **Cooking's figures from several sources**: reconciled, then saved before a compaction, and finished in the sixty-fifth pass (below), decided again by a rule written down first. Two blind reviewers gathered 519 figures (A 302, B 217), among them USDA's own measured-cup study (HERR 41, 1969) and USDA's Foundation Foods samples; their estimates agree within a gram for most ingredients, and both found the same large corrections to SR Legacy (cake flour 137 → 112 g, bread flour 137 → 123 g, active dry yeast 192 → 147 g, baking powder 221 → 192 g, baking soda 221 → 239 g, shortening 205 → 188 g). Written: core's `ingredients.ts` (method, spread, sources, note; `byDensity` for liquids), the stick and the packet exact by their packages' net weights, the note under the table and About's lines, the tests, `docs/research/cooking-2.md` with its files in `cooking-2/`, ADR 0021 amended, UX-SPEC, UNITS-CATALOG, TESTING, the audit, cooking-1.md marked superseded. Gate and all 253 browser tests pass. Waiting on the third reviewer's citation check before it ships.

## Sixty-fifth pass: sound sources, and a rule to validate by

Follows the sixty-fourth, after a compaction; live: `1cb83c8`, Cooking's new figures not yet shipped. Sean brought two outside takes on cooking sources, a search assistant's list of "the ultimate" sources (USDA FoodData Central's portions, USDA Handbook 102, NIST Handbook 133, _The Book of Yields_, _The Professional Chef_, King Arthur's chart, America's Test Kitchen) and an editor's critique of it, open to pushback: "just want to make sure we have very sound and well sourced data... a good plan in place and logic so we know how to validate and come up with our own best conclusion", with a feedback section later for visitors to report conflicting data, and more research where it is needed.

| Observation                                    | Plan                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| ---------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| The outside list: better sources?              | Each weighed in `cooking-2.md`, "Sources considered and set aside": FoodData Central used, but SR Legacy not counted (its cups' methods unstated, several a dipped cup's); Handbook 102 is yields after trimming and cooking, another question; Handbook 133 checks packages' net contents, standing only behind the stick's and the packet's weights; the textbooks not read, so not counted; King Arthur and America's Test Kitchen one weighing each, neither the authority. The critique was right but for one point: citing a figure read by its page is fine; copying a whole table is what isn't. |
| Logic to validate by                           | The rule written down first and made a test: the weighings that count (the method, the food, read on their page, each source once), their median, with fewer than three USDA's measured cups, a liquid by its density; core's `cupFigure` recomputes every figure from its sources.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| The citation check (the sixty-fourth's item 6) | 192 citations: 170 confirmed, 21 corrected, 1 not reached, none wrong; every correction applied (America's Test Kitchen's flour and sugar cups dipped, USDA's 1969 shortening and peanut butter spooned, Milk Street's flour table an average, Foundation Foods' peanut butter SR Legacy's one weighing, America's Test Kitchen's chopped pecans 119 g).                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Figures found but never cited                  | Screened by the rule; the six that would count read the same day: Gold Medal's, Beranbaum's third, and King Arthur's baking soda counted; Erik Jacobsen's not shown to be weighed; Allrecipes refused requests; the Food Buying Guide's nut butters one figure for all.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Re-opening, and the feedback to come           | A visitor's report is a lead, screened like any source; a new weighing or a changed product re-opens one ingredient; where the sources cannot settle it, our own weighing by a written protocol (`cooking-2.md`). Six figures rest on fewer than three weighings: baking powder, almond flour, cake flour, cornstarch, whole wheat flour, yogurt.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| For Sean, optional                             | Weighing those six in his kitchen by the protocol (a 0.1 g scale, ten cups each), or saying whether he owns _The Book of Yields_. Not needed to ship.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |

**Built the same day**, on the gate: core's ingredients carry their counted weighings as data (`CupSource`), with `cupFigure` and `cupBasis` in core and a test that recomputes every figure; About's lines say how each figure was reached, and the line under the table how many weighings; `cooking-2.md` rewritten around the rule, with `cooking-2/sources.md` listing every figure screened and `check.md`/`check.csv` kept; ADR 0021, UX-SPEC, UNITS-CATALOG, TESTING, the audit, and a learning. Eleven figures moved from the first reconciliation, all-purpose flour 125 → 127 g and baking powder 192 → 177 g the largest; from SR Legacy, the figures live until now, eleven by more than 3%.

The premise his phone tests: the slivers were bands left outside as a change ended, and since `3eb150a` the envelope brings every band back inside before it ends. A sliver puts Glitch alone back in the clipped set (one id). Then measured: putting the clip back at the settle re-rasterized the picture's edges in both engines (140 to 470 pixels, up to 108 levels), the lift over the neighbors costing nothing; so the clip now toggles only at a change's start, never at a settle (`038879a`; a learning), and the last frame equals the settled picture again |

| Horizontal scroll of the bar on a phone instead of the wrap?                                   | Recommended against, in the reply: a scrolling bar is a scroll container and would clip the category menu that opens from it; what scrolls out of view is undiscoverable without a cue; and sideways scrolling of content that can wrap is what WCAG's reflow rule asks to avoid, while the wrapped line costs nothing once it scrolls away. If one row on a phone matters, the standard shape is an overflow menu holding what does not fit (undo, redo, clear all), measured, not a scroll. His call                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |

## Seventy-fifth pass: the layout kept, the email kept simple, the feedback record, the form's fields (2026-10-17)

Follows the seventy-fourth; live: `c182584`. Sean: really happy with the layout, great work; 16 px kept, 24 looked off beside the rest. Next the logic of the email and the feedback. He had not realized a visitor gets no follow-up email; since that is so, nothing to overcomplicate now. A domain address to reply from would be nice, but for a small experimental site a Gmail is fine, his own for now. Can a submission also be logged in a database, Supabase perhaps, so every message is stored and I can analyze them, break down what they say, see the overlap? If real traffic exceeds the limits, re-evaluate; for now: a visitor leaves feedback, it is emailed to him, and we store and categorize it to draw conclusions. The form: the email field optional. On his phone, tapping into the email or the message zooms the page in and hides what he types: no zoom, or the best-practice way. About's layout after these.

| ------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| The layout                     | Kept (`5c7a4e9`), no longer a trial; the docs say so                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |

Follows the seventy-fifth; live: `6420abb`. Sean: the analysis sounds good, but he wants to see the senders' emails too, who writes and how, so nothing anonymized or hidden from him. The confirmation should read "Thank you for your feedback!". The zoom fix: whatever is best practice and scalable, a smooth consistent experience. His anchor nav meant a vertical nav on the left that scrolls each section into focus; not now. A side note: with No limit on rounding, why are Nearest, Up, and Down disabled? A value still gets cut off somewhere, so the rule still applies.

| Observation                     | Plan, and what changed                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| ------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |

If he would rather have them in the committed copy too, the repo is private and it is one line to change                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| "Thank you for your feedback!"  | The sent line (this pass's second commit); the failed line unchanged                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| The zoom, made a rule           | One recipe already fixes every text field, and a browser test on the phone project now holds every drawn text field on every page, the labs' and the gallery's too, to 16 px, so nothing new can zoom (the same commit)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| About's tabs                    | Built as a trial (this pass's first commit): a `Tabs` component on the WAI-ARIA pattern, text tabs on a hairline with the links' accent line under the open one, one Tab stop with ← → Home End, the panels named by their tabs and hidden when not open; the open tab in the address's hash and a hash opening its tab once hydrated, the row brought into view. About: the sketchbook as is, then the page's column (`ABOUT_COLUMN`, 672 px, the header on it too) with the tabs on its left edge, Feedback open first, its form 448 px wide on the left, and Every formula's lines, bar, and rows on the left under the tab, its heading gone. A sample in the gallery. The formulas' tests open the tab by its hash; a new About spec covers the tabs. Outcome: kept in the eighty-first pass (`8829c38`) |
| His anchor nav                  | A vertical nav at the left that scrolls each section into focus, as docs sites have: noted for later, not explored                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| No limit and the rounding modes | He is right. No limit is not unlimited: it is the table's six significant digits, so the last digit is a rounding too. The modes are off with it only because that path formats with the engine's nearest-only formatter (`formatNumber`), while Up and Down live in `formatPlaces` and the fractions. A mode-aware significant-digits formatter in core, with its golden tests, lets the modes stay on with No limit: a small change, recommended, on his go. Outcome: his go, built in the seventy-seventh pass (`0cfba80`)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Then                            | His verdict on the tabs on the live site; the privacy line; `/tidy full`; Search Console, Bing, Porkbun                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |

## Seventy-ninth pass: the column's step when a panel brings the scrollbar (2026-10-18)

Follows the seventy-eighth. Sean: when the calculator, Units, or Display toggles the browser's scroll, the main column shifts a little to the left; it should stay anchored, accommodating the vertical scrollbar; is this expected and best practice, bug or not, no hackish fix. | Observation       | Plan, and what changed                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| The column's step | Expected browser behavior, not a bug of ours: a classic scrollbar (Windows, Linux) takes 15 to 17 px of layout width the moment the page outgrows the window, and a centered column re-centers in what is left, half the bar's width to the left. The standard remedy, `scrollbar-gutter: stable` on `html`, keeps the bar's room whether or not it shows, so nothing moves; the price is an empty strip where the bar would be on a page shorter than the window, on classic-scrollbar systems alone. Applied; a smoke check reads the rule and holds the header's edge across a panel opening (headless Chromium draws no classic bar; a probe showed the gutter reserving 15 px) |
| The empty states  | Five examples per kind offered in chat, each keeping the kind's job (an inaccuracy: the value seen, the one expected, the source; a bug: the device, the action, what happened; an idea: one wish in a line), each set across categories; his picks pending. ## Eighty-second pass: the tab that sent the page to its top (2026-10-18)

Follows the eighty-first. Sean, on his phone: tapping Every formula shoots to the top of the page instead of keeping the scroll position; a bug, and the logic should be consistent best practice.

| Observation | Plan, and what changed                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| ----------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| The jump    | A bug, WebKit's: Chromium kept the scroll as a phone and a desktop, WebKit as an iPhone sent the page to 0 from its foot. The switch hides one panel and shows the other in one render, but WebKit lays out between the two and clamps the scroll while the page is briefly as short as the tabs. Not the hash update (the same with it stubbed). Fixed with the pattern the formulas' list already had: `keeping(anchor, change)`, now shared in lib/keeping.ts, the switch rendered at once with `flushSync` and the row's place restored; verified in WebKit as an iPhone. The About test holds the row's place from the page's foot; a learning says Chromium's scroll anchoring hides this class of fault, so WebKit is the check |
| Then        | `/tidy full`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |

Same with hovering over Reset order, Show hidden units, the copy icon; what are the best practice standards here? Seems the About page, the home page, and the interactions don't use it consistently; if there's a good reason, or best UX, let me know, otherwise help me explore whether this is a bug" | Audited. Tailwind v4's preflight leaves a `button` on the browser's default cursor, the arrow (v3 gave buttons the hand), so every `Button` (the toolbar's tools, undo, redo, clear, the copy buttons, Send, Flip and Pause, the calculator's keys, Units' handles and its Reset order and Show hidden), the tabs (buttons with `role="tab"`), and the two menus' triggers show the arrow, while links show the hand by the browser's own rule and the class lists that carry `cursor-pointer` do too: the table's rows and the start's tiles (`CURSOR_ROW`), the menus' choices and the settings' rows (`OPTION_BASE`), Every formula's categories (`DISCLOSURE`), the segmented controls, and the drag handles their grab hand. So it is neither convention: the platforms (Apple's and Windows' guidelines, and browsers) give the hand to links alone and buttons the arrow; the web's own practice (Material, Bootstrap's reboot, GitHub's Primer, most sites) gives the hand to everything that answers a click; what every guide agrees on is one rule throughout, which the site lacks. Recommendation: the web's rule, since the rows and segments already have the hand and nothing here is a native app: one base rule in `global.css` giving every enabled `button`, `[role="button"]`, `[role="tab"]`, and `summary` the hand (`aria-disabled` and `disabled` kept on the arrow, `cursor-not-allowed` dropped from the segmented control's disabled state for the same arrow), the class lists keeping theirs for rows that are not buttons; a smoke test reading every visible control's computed cursor on each page; a "Cursors" paragraph in docs/DESIGN-SYSTEM.md; a learning on v4's preflight. His call; nothing changed yet |
| Found answering 4: the snap before rounding drops true digits of large values                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | `rounded()` in core's rounded.ts snaps a scaled value to 12 significant digits before rounding it, so float noise never rounds a typed value down a step (0.29 down stays 0.29). With two places, a value from 1e10 up has more than 12 digits once scaled, and the snap throws the rest away: 1 light-year in km writes 9,460,730,472,580.00 for 9,460,730,472,580.80, and 1 parsec in km 30,856,775,814,900.00 for 30,856,775,814,913.67, read from core's own `formatPlaces` on 2026-10-18; 12,345,678,901.23 writes .20. Proposed: round the number itself and forgive only the float's last-bit noise, a snap to the nearest 15-digit decimal taken only when the scaled value sits within a few ulps of it (the 0.29 and 1.005 cases) and left alone otherwise, so the light-year and the parsec keep their digits to the double's last; the 1e15 limit stays (no hundredths exist there, the double's step being 0.125), and past it the six significant digits take the chosen mode, as No limit does (`firstWritten` falling through to `formatSignificant`); tests for each case; UX-SPEC §7's sentence. Sean asked to see the reasoning before anything changes: his call                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |

Expected: a lowercase word sits on the baseline inside a box sized by the font's ascent and descent, as on every site. The standard fix is `text-box: trim-both cap alphabetic`, which trims the box to the cap height and the baseline so padding centers the ink, a progressive standard (Chromium 133, Safari 18.2; Firefox ignores it). The cause is the shape of the process: CI ran on every push to main and Vercel's Deployment Checks waited for both jobs (ADR 0008), so every commit pushed on its own was a 12 minute run and a 12 minute wait, and the day's work pushed per commit. The local gate (the ship skill) already ran the same checks before every push, on Windows; CI's own catch was Linux and the phone emulation (one find this month, `4a70d40`). Three ways were laid out, the recommendation first: A, no checks on a push, the workflow by hand only, the checks local before every push as always; B, the verify job alone as the deployment gate; C, both kept and made cheap. Sean chose A: written in Cursor at his direction and committed here, `8e0c883` (ADR 0008 amended; the ship skill, CLAUDE.md, docs/DEPLOYMENT.md, docs/TESTING.md, and a learning), the Vercel Deployment Checks list emptied by Sean before the push                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |

## Ninety-fourth pass: Search Console, Bing, and the owner's page back (2026-10-19)

Follows the tidy after the ninety-third. Live at `0f95dcf`. Sean set up Google Search Console and Bing Webmaster Tools at his screen, step by step in chat (docs/DEPLOYMENT.md, "Search"), then asked about Umami.

| Observation                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Outcome                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| "Sometimes my IP changes on my phone or desktop, and for Umami I do need an easy way to make sure whatever browsers I'm testing are not counted; is it best to bring back that page which has a button I can click, or what do you recommend?" Then: "will the button state be updated and know if the browser is flagged or not, or will I always need to reclick it? Just don't want it to give me a wrong indicator if I've already signaled a browser not to monitor; otherwise that sounds good" | The exclusion is the browser's storage, never the IP, so a changed IP keeps it; what loses it is clearing the site's data and, on his iPhone, Safari deleting a site's storage after seven days without a tap on it, quietly. The page's line reads the storage each time it loads, so it cannot show a stale answer, and the button is a toggle. **Outcome** (2026-10-19, the same day): the compound build landed (ADR 0026). Core reads a compound as one quantity (`compound.ts`, called by `parse`; the worker's must-read and must-not lists as its tests), five pairs are units of their own, each leading its section, written as their parts with the total rounded and then split (`formatPair`), and the About page writes their formulas from core; the web offers a compound's pair as the match Enter takes, shows the quiet line under the box (every compound read, the decimal of feet's pair one press away, as recommended), carries the compound in the URL in the pair's words, moves a pair row into the box as its parts, and counts an unread input by its shape (decision 7). One browser test walks the height from `5'11` to the quiet line's press and a pair row taken into the box. Three things seen on the built page are Sean's to decide ("Open with Sean" in CLAUDE.md): the pair row's last part at Display's places like every decimal (`5 ft 11.00 in` at two places, where the mock showed `5 ft 11 in`); the copy button covering the tail of the longest name on the active row, which the pair's row now is right after Enter (`feet and inch`), as it covered `nautical mil` before; and at a phone's width the pair's long names (`pounds and ounces`) beside a micrograms value (`71,667,594,460.00`) sending Weight's names to give way, where `milligrams` fit before.

The notation is data on the pair (how its parts attach), `formatPair` writes it, the row and a row moved into the box use it, and the URL keeps the canonical words. ## Ninety-ninth pass: Sean's yeses on the pair row, the copy by a double-tap, and where the pair rows sit (2026-10-19; built the same day)

Follows the ninety-eighth. His answers to its plans, and one more question. Outcome, the same day, on his yeses ("I like the double tap suggestion; yes, that is expected and the better UX: tap once, it goes in the box, tap again, it copies; a double-tap on an already selected item copies; the one undo step, the flash, and a shortcut. For the separated Mixed units I like this approach a lot. No visible affordance for mouse users, we just make the hover button gone"): the copy by a second tap or click and the Mixed units section built as recommended, the hover button gone, the shortcuts map naming the double-click beside Ctrl C. Left for a piece of its own: an entry point to the gestures on a phone, where the shortcuts map hides (its icon needs a header 384 px wide, and a phone's is 358), likely a tool at the toolbar's end on a touch screen.

| "We initially had room reserved after the row for the button, but the dead space, or seeing all buttons at once, is not aesthetic or ideal; and it's still not clear how often users will want to copy values from the data table, it seems a nice-to-have. A single copy in the input would at least give users the ability without overloading or obscuring the table, but the box already has the clear × and the chip with its ×, which is heavy. Maybe, if we have a few phone shortcuts, a phone-shortcuts button like the keyboard-shortcuts one, so users can find the controls if they wish" | Recommended, one gesture everywhere and no button in the table: a double-tap on a phone, a double-click with a pointer, copies the row as it shows, the first tap taking the row into the box as today and the second copying, so a double-tap on another unit's row makes it the box's and copies it (one undo step; a tap's action can never wait for a second tap without lag), the check flashing at the row's end for two seconds and the status saying Copied; Ctrl/⌘+C stays the keys' way; the hover button goes, so nothing covers a name and nothing reserves room. The shortcuts map gains a Touch section (tap a row to use its unit, double-tap to copy its value, Enter on a phone's keyboard puts it away) and its icon shows on a phone where the header has the room, measured as its icons' hiding is. The alternative kept if a pointer needs a visible affordance: the hover button in the row's spare middle, between the value and the unit, where a desktop row has the room and never a name. Not sure how we'd create a non-confusing interaction where sometimes we surface it and other times we don't, or a sound logic for it; but the compound naming structure messes with the alignment of everything else and forces you to scan it instead of quickly scanning single units when you may not be interested in compound units. Help me think through that"                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | Recommended: the pair rows in a section of their own, "Mixed units", after the systems and Other and before Specialty, where Pace sits, so the single units' sections read as before and whoever wants the mixed form finds it under its heading, every time, in every category that has one (Length, Weight, Time, Angle); its rows the bare value, no name, since the value names itself (`5′ 11″`, `11 st 4 lb`, `158 lb 0 oz`, `1:30:00`, `45° 30′ 15″`), which also takes the pairs' long names (`hours, minutes, and seconds`) out of the name column that every section shares, so the single rows' alignment is theirs again; the names stay in the chip, the matches, Edit, and Hidden; a Mixed units switch in Units, on in every preset, off by the visitor who never wants it, as Specialty's is, and Edit hiding a pair on its own as any unit. Set aside: surfacing a pair only when the box's unit is its own (the height answer to 180 cm, the row's reason, would never show), or only in a range of values (a rule nobody could predict). Sean's call: the section, its name (`Mixed units`, or `Compound`), and the switch's default |

In any case we need at least the symbol to appear as expected; this is best UX, right? I think we really need to not just show the value alone; I was wrong to go with that in the table: we need the value and units in the table for sure. Maybe just as is, having the symbol and name and the value to the left like we do everything else, is best. Ideally a systematic way that does not stray far from how we do other values and units, or even matches; a little stray is okay" | The pair row as every row: its symbol in the symbol column (`ft in`, `st lb`, `lb oz`, `h min s`, `° ′ ″`) beside the value in its marks, then its name; the symbol in the sizer's stack as any, so nothing moves; the name still the first to give way, and the symbol the second, before any single unit's name: measured on the build, Time's `h min s`, the widest symbol, had cost its single rows their names on WebKit at his phone's width, so on a narrow phone Time's pair row keeps its value alone (`1:30:00`, which carries its units) while Length's, Weight's, and Angle's keep their symbols (`ft in` with its name, `st lb`, `lb oz`, `° ′ ″`); Symbol only shows the symbol. The chip, the match, and the row now read alike. Built the same day. |

Set aside: wrapping (the rows keep to one line, 44 px each, the fit's rule since 2026-10-08), and `&` or `+` in the names (a style the other names do not have, for two letters). Offered: the three-part names without their "and" (`hours, minutes, seconds`, `degrees, minutes, seconds`), as settings and maps write them, a little shorter; his call.                                                                                                         |

Follows the hundred-and-first. Live at `cfeca0d` and `1adb2bc`: the pair row with its symbol, the name giving way first and the symbol second. His feedback from Cursor with the calculator or Units open beside the table, and from his phone, in four messages.

| Observation                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Plan                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| "Is this a bug? When a panel is open I don't see symbol or name; we should match the convention and have the symbol, right? Same with hours, minutes, seconds: we should at least show the symbols, and instead of hiding the label fully, at least truncate it to indicate there is more of the label that can't be seen; with the symbol, the user can infer what the label is"         | A bug in the fit: it hid its columns in a fixed order and never looked back, so once the names had gone for room the pairs' symbols stayed hidden although the row had room to spare. The fit now decides each column from the last to give way, shown whenever the grid fits with it and every column before it hidden, so the pair's symbol comes back the moment the names go. And the pair's name is cut short with an ellipsis rather than hidden, as he asked, the symbol beside it saying the rest. Built the same day; the learning logged.                                                                                                                                                         |
| "We do have the wrap functionality, right? Would it be better practice to have the symbol's font size match the top value on the top line and be aligned with it, so the sub-value sits below? Or maybe it's already constructed as best practice and doesn't need solving just now"                                                                                                      | Left as built for now, as he said. Recommendation when he wants it: in the stacked state the symbol and the name sit beside the first line, the decimal's, which the unit belongs to, the fraction a sub-line under the value; one small change (the tall cells aligned to the start with the first line's leading). Open in CLAUDE.md.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| "For the compound symbol, can't we come up with something where the font shrinks and wraps comfortably, still fitting one row, or a wrapped row if the values call for it? Something aesthetic that works"                                                                                                                                                                                | Neither shrinks nor wraps: the pair's symbol takes the spare width before the symbols, right-aligned with them, never widening their column, and gives way only where even that lacks the room, coming back once the names go; a symbol cut short or wrapped would read as another symbol, and a smaller one as another kind of thing. Measured on the build at his phone's width: every pair row keeps its symbol with the single names beside it, except Time's on WebKit, where even the spare width could not hold `h min s`; so the time pair's symbol is `h:m:s`, the clock's own shape, the grammar already reading `m` as a minute after an hour, and `h min s` stays an alias. Built the same day. |
| "Yes, let's remove the 'and' and use commas to separate"                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | The three-part names: `hours, minutes, seconds` and `degrees, minutes, seconds`, the old names kept as aliases, so typing either still finds them. The two-part names keep their "and" (`feet and inches`), what people say and search; `feet, inches` is his to ask for. Built the same day.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| "If we have table data, the longest non-compound unit should determine the width of the right column we size against; that way, if the compound doesn't fit, it knows when to truncate or wrap. Instead of the longest absolute term determining the width, since the compound will almost always win, the compound reacts based on the other units, not the units based on the compound" | Adopted as the rule (ADR 0026 amended): the sizer's stacks hold the single units alone; the pair's symbol spans the spare width before the symbols; the pair's name is sized as if empty (`contain: inline-size`) and cut short. Nothing a pair shows ever moves a single row. Built the same day.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| "A bug: a double-tap on mobile, maybe elsewhere, makes the text stretch out across the row when copying, then doesn't revert to its proper position until moving to another row, and sometimes messes with the alignment of the other values and rows"                                                                                                                                    | Reproduced on Playwright's WebKit at his phone's size, step by step: the copied check, an absolutely positioned child of the row's subgrid, sent the value left and the symbol and name right the moment it appeared, and the row stayed so after the flash; Chromium was fine. The flash is now a grid item laid on the row's own area, no positioned child in any subgrid, and a touch test on WebKit compares the row's cells with its neighbour's after a double-tap; the rows also select no word on a double-tap. Built the same day; the learning logged.                                                                                                                                            |

## Hundred-and-third pass: Sean's follow-up on the labels, a clock typed alone, and what gives way first (2026-10-20; built the same day)

Follows the hundred-and-second. Live at `146f4f0`: the single units sizing the columns, the pair's symbol over the spare width and giving way first, its name cut short, the flash a grid item. His notes from Cursor, and the context meter at 44 %.

| Observation                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Plan                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| "For the two-part names also remove the 'and' and just use a comma; that should be our convention for our mixed units, for space conservation in general; we can re-evaluate if we need to"                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Every pair's name is its parts' names with commas between: `feet, inches`, `stone, pounds`, `pounds, ounces`, with the three-part names; the old names stay as aliases, so typing them still finds the pair. Built the same day.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| "When typing a time, should we give users the shorthand for the options? 5:55 should show the selectable units, h m s or pace or anything else; it turns up blank, where 5'11 shows the possible units for feet and inches"                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Yes: a clock typed alone names its units, as a compound names its pair. `5:55` offers the units written as a clock, the time pair (`h:m:s`) first, then the paces (`min/km`, `min/mi`), each with its category, and Enter takes the first; a unit typed after it, or in the box, takes the clock as a number, as before. Built the same day.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| "For miles, why does 5:5 show 5.00 but 5:51 shows 5.85? Expected, a bug, or am I missing something?"                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | A gap in the grammar: a clock's parts after the first had to be two digits, so `5:5` read as 5 with `:5` left over, silently. A part is now one digit or two: `5:5` is 5:05 (5.08), on its way to 5:55 or not. Built the same day.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| "Using the single units as the measure: is this best practice and UX, or are there alternatives? Testing it, the truncation works, but if we wrap instead maybe that is better; either way we need a rule for the compound that may vary a little, logical and matching how usability is best served. One inconsistency already: when things get tight we drop the compound symbol first instead of its name, so it behaves differently from everything else; what is our best strategy?" | He is right, and the rule is now one for every row: a symbol never gives way, a name goes when the row lacks the room, and a pair's name is cut short rather than hidden only because it never sized its column. A pair's symbol must fit over the spare width, and where it lacks the room the names go for it, exactly as they go for a wide value; nothing of a pair drops before the single units' equivalent would. Truncation kept over wrapping: a wrapped name would make the pair rows taller than the rest, the symbol beside the cut name says what it is, and the full name is in the chip, the matches, Edit, and for screen readers. Built the same day. |

## Hundred-and-fourth pass: Sean's questions on the clock's order, the lone foot mark, and Enter twice to copy (2026-10-20; built the same day)

Follows the hundred-and-third. Live at `fe5e879`: commas in every pair's name, a pair's symbol never giving way, a clock typed alone naming its units, `5:5` as 5:05. | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |

If so, shouldn't that show first as priority?"                                      | Yes, by the clock's shape: two parts are minutes and seconds, so the paces come first, the visitor's own first (the mile's where the region is the US or imperial), then the time pair; three parts (`1:30:00`) are hours, minutes, seconds, so the pair comes first; in Speed or Time that category leads, as matches always come. Built the same day.                                                                                          |
| "I believe before, typing 5' would also show ft in as a possibility; did we remove that or did I imagine it? Now ft in only shows if I type 5'1. Expected or a bug, and what is best practice?"                        | Never built: the grammar's must-not list has `6'` as six feet, so `5'` matched feet alone and the pair came at the second part. Best practice, built the same day: feet stays the match Enter takes (`5'` is five feet), and the pair the mark opens is offered after it, in case the inches follow, so the height's pair is in view from the first mark and nothing jumps as it is typed; `5′`, the typographic prime, finds feet as `5'` does. |
| "Can we also make hitting Enter twice on the row value trigger the copy? Then in the keyboard shortcuts, change double-click to something like 2× tap or tap tap, as two separate keys; which do you think is better?" | Enter twice copies: the first Enter takes the row, a second within the double-tap window copies it, as a second tap or click does, one rule for every pointer and key. The map shows each as the two presses it is, two keycaps (Enter Enter, click click), never a count and never a chord: the map is the desktop's, where a mouse clicks, and the phone's own section, when it comes (open with Sean), says tap tap. Built the same day.      |

## Tidy pass, after the hundred-and-fifth (2026-10-20)

Sean, 2026-10-20, after compacting: "is it worth it to do a tidy at all to make sure we are clean and doc learnings etc, or is this something we are measuring and will make sure as we build out the agent orchestrator/research etc skill that we include as like a verifier, or way to validate, or maybe it's not part of the skill but we run a full tidy after we finish all our tasks? Just want to make sure learnings and best practices etc are documented, and that our skill is being built in tandem too for the division of agent tasks. I will defer to your best strategy"; and: "after we do a lot of the agent research/building, want to do an accuracy pass just to make sure our data is sound, conversions solid, like a final touch-up or clean-up to validate". Scope: the twenty-four commits since the tidy after the ninety-fourth (`52d313b`): the demand map's three waves, the compound build and its eight passes of revision, the copy gestures, the clock's offers. Baseline: the gate green at `2472bb9`, the suite green in four projects (346 passed, 65 skipped by project).

| Area  | What changed                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |

Not folded into the research or orchestrate skills: they verify facts, the tidy verifies the system, and both skills already carry their own lessons from the first run (the shared scratchpad, the report as text)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Found | A pair's line in Edit and Hidden drew its name alone, the ninety-eighth pass's exception left when the chip, the matches, and the row got their symbols back; with Symbol only the line was a checkbox and nothing visible. Fixed in a commit of its own, the symbol then the name as every line's (`ba0b229`), kept by Sean the same day; the learning: a reversed rule's exceptions are searched for by their condition on every surface (docs/LEARNINGS.md, the tidy skill). And, from the height test looping every theme: on the phone project (412 px) in the two monospace themes, Neo-noir and Hardhat, Time's single rows lose their names with the clock's pair in the table, since `h:m:s` needs 55 px where the spare width beside the names leaves 43; the sans and serif themes keep them. The hundred-and-third pass's rule, measured, not a fault: recorded in the design system ("A cell that reacts to its column"), the test pinning the rule in every theme, the alternative (the symbol stepping down the type ladder before the names go) not taken: Sean kept the rule the same day |
